# Module I: 250 Everyday German Sayings

## Greetings, Goodbyes & Pleasantries

**#1. Hallo!** — Hello!
*Example sentence:* Hallo, wie geht es dir? (Hello, how are you?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Hallo* (Hello) + *wie* (how) + *geht* (goes) + *es* (it) + *dir* (to you [informal dative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Hello, how does it go to you?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Hello, how are you doing?"
  - *Context:* German uses the impersonal dative expression *wie geht es [Person in Dativ]* to ask about someone's well-being, rather than "how are you" with the verb *to be*.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Informal interjection / greeting. The example sentence includes a W-question introduced by the question word *wie*, followed by the finite verb *geht* in position 2 (V2), dummy subject *es*, and personal pronoun *dir* in the Dative case.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 & 2. Universally used informal greeting among peers, friends, classmates, and family members. Avoid when addressing examiners directly unless invited to use informal register.

**#2. Guten Morgen!** — Good morning!
*Example sentence:* Guten Morgen, haben Sie gut geschlafen? (Good morning, did you sleep well?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Guten Morgen* (Good morning) + *haben Sie* (have you [formal]) + *gut* (well) + *geschlafen* (slept [past participle of *schlafen*]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Good morning, have you well slept?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Good morning, did you sleep well?"
  - *Context:* Polite morning inquiry asked to guests, hotel customers, or colleagues after a night's rest.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Elliptical greeting for the complete clause "[Ich wünsche Ihnen einen] guten Morgen" (Accusative masculine: *guten* + *der Morgen*). The example features a Yes/No question with the auxiliary verb *haben* in position 1, formal subject pronoun *Sie*, adverb *gut*, and the past participle (Partizip II) *geschlafen* placed at the clause end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen (greeting examiners upon entering the room) and Hören. Customarily used from early morning until roughly 10:00–11:00 AM. Appropriate in both formal and informal contexts.

**#3. Guten Tag!** — Good day! / Hello!
*Example sentence:* Guten Tag, mein Name ist Müller. (Good day, my name is Müller.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Guten Tag* (Good day) + *mein Name* (my name) + *ist* (is) + *Müller* (Müller [surname]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Good day, my name is Müller."
  - *Context:* Standard formal daytime greeting and self-introduction used in business, administrative offices, or doctor's practices.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Accusative masculine greeting formula (*einen guten Tag*). The declarative clause follows standard V2 word order with the copular verb *ist* in position 2, possessive determiner *mein* (Nominative masculine), and subject *mein Name*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (self-introduction) and Teil 2/3. The benchmark polite daytime greeting (used from approx. 11:00 AM until nightfall) with officials, doctors, shop assistants, and examiners.

**#4. Guten Abend!** — Good evening!
*Example sentence:* Guten Abend, willkommen im Restaurant. (Good evening, welcome to the restaurant.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Guten Abend* (Good evening) + *willkommen* (welcome) + *im* (*in* + *dem* = in the [dative masculine]) + *Restaurant* (restaurant).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Good evening, welcome to the restaurant."
  - *Context:* Standard hospitable welcome spoken by waitstaff or hosts when patrons arrive for dinner.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Accusative masculine formula (*einen guten Abend*). Followed by the welcoming formula *willkommen* and the contracted prepositional phrase *im* (*in* + *dem*, Dative neuter: *das Restaurant*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen & Hören. The standard formal and informal greeting used from roughly 18:00 onwards. Frequently tested in audio tracks simulating dining or evening events.

**#5. Gute Nacht!** — Good night!
*Example sentence:* Gute Nacht, schlaf gut! (Good night, sleep well!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Gute Nacht* (Good night) + *schlaf* (sleep! [informal singular imperative of *schlafen*]) + *gut* (well).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Good night, sleep well!"
  - *Context:* Said exclusively right before heading to bed to friends, family members, or flatmates.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Accusative feminine greeting formula (*eine gute Nacht*, *die Nacht* -> feminine adjective ending *-e*). Followed by an informal singular imperative (*schlaf!* from *schlafen*, formed from the verb stem without *-st* and dropping the umlaut).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Hören & everyday communication. Exclusively used as a parting phrase before retiring to bed, never as an arrival greeting in the evening.

**#6. Tschüss!** — Bye!
*Example sentence:* Tschüss, bis morgen! (Bye, see you tomorrow!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Tschüss* (Bye / Ciao) + *bis* (until) + *morgen* (tomorrow).
  - *Literal translation:* "Bye, until tomorrow!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Bye, see you tomorrow!"
  - *Context:* Everyday casual goodbye among classmates, coworkers, and friends.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Informal interjection/parting phrase. Paired with preposition *bis* (taking adverbs of time or accusative noun phrases) and temporal adverb *morgen*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1–3. The most widespread informal farewell in modern German across all German-speaking regions.

**#7. Auf Wiedersehen!** — Goodbye! (formal)
*Example sentence:* Auf Wiedersehen, Herr Schmidt! (Goodbye, Mr. Schmidt!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Auf* (upon / to) + *Wiedersehen* (seeing again [neuter noun]) + *Herr Schmidt* (Mr. Schmidt).
  - *Literal translation:* "Upon seeing again, Mr. Schmidt!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Goodbye, Mr. Schmidt!"
  - *Context:* Standard respectful formal parting phrase in face-to-face encounters.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Prepositional formula consisting of preposition *auf* (governing accusative) + nominalized infinitive *Wiedersehen* (neuter noun, capitalized).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen (used when concluding the oral exam and departing from the examiners) and Schreiben (formal letter/email closings). Required in all formal interactions.

**#8. Bis bald!** — See you soon!
*Example sentence:* Ich muss gehen. Bis bald! (I have to go. See you soon!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich* (I) + *muss* (must / have to [modal *müssen*]) + *gehen* (to go/leave) + *Bis* (until) + *bald* (soon).
  - *Literal translation:* "I must go. Until soon!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "I have to leave now. See you soon!"
  - *Context:* Used when departing an informal social gathering while anticipating seeing the person in the near future.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Main clause featuring modal verb *muss* in position 2 and infinitive *gehen* at the sentence bracket end. Parting phrase pairs preposition *bis* with temporal adverb *bald*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Schreiben (closing informal emails to friends) and Sprechen. Suitable when a future meeting is expected within a short time frame.

**#9. Bis morgen!** — See you tomorrow!
*Example sentence:* Der Kurs ist fertig. Bis morgen! (The class is over. See you tomorrow!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Der Kurs* (The course/class [masculine]) + *ist* (is) + *fertig* (finished/done) + *Bis* (until) + *morgen* (tomorrow).
  - *Literal translation:* "The course is finished. Until tomorrow!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Class is over. See you tomorrow!"
  - *Context:* Spoken by students or teachers when concluding a language lesson.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Declarative clause with masculine subject *Der Kurs*, copular verb *ist* in V2, and predicate adjective *fertig*. Followed by *bis* + temporal adverb *morgen* (lowercase adverb "tomorrow", distinct from capitalized noun *der Morgen* "morning").
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen & Schreiben. Very common in language course situations, office chats, and daily school routines.

**#10. Bis später!** — See you later!
*Example sentence:* Ich gehe jetzt einkaufen. Bis später! (I'm going shopping now. See you later!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich* (I) + *gehe* (go) + *jetzt* (now) + *einkaufen* (to shop / grocery shopping) + *Bis* (until) + *später* (later).
  - *Literal translation:* "I go now shopping. Until later!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "I'm heading out to do some shopping now. See you later!"
  - *Context:* Leaving flatmates or family for a brief errand with the expectation of meeting again later that day.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + finite verb *gehe* in V2 + temporal adverb *jetzt* + infinitive *einkaufen* at the end. Parting phrase pairs *bis* with comparative adverb *später* (from *spät*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen. Specifically indicates that you expect to see the interlocutor again later on the same day.

**#11. Bitte!** — Please! / You're welcome!
*Example sentence:* Kann ich bitte ein Glas Wasser haben? (Can I please have a glass of water?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Kann ich* (Can I) + *bitte* (please) + *ein Glas Wasser* (a glass [of] water [neuter accusative]) + *haben* (have).
  - *Literal translation:* "Can I please a glass water have?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "May I please have a glass of water?"
  - *Context:* Standard polite formula for requesting a beverage at a restaurant, office, or host's home.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Politeness particle/interjection. In the example, modal verb *Kann* takes position 1, subject *ich*, polite adverb *bitte*, direct object *ein Glas Wasser* (Accusative neuter), and infinitive *haben* at the end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 (formulating polite requests / "Bitten formulieren und darauf reagieren"). Indispensable in polite German speech.

**#12. Danke!** — Thank you!
*Example sentence:* Danke, das ist sehr nett von Ihnen. (Thank you, that is very nice of you.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Danke* (Thank you) + *das* (that) + *ist* (is) + *sehr nett* (very kind/nice) + *von Ihnen* (of you [formal dative]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Thank you, that is very kind of you."
  - *Context:* Gracious formal acknowledgment when receiving help, a compliment, or a favor from an adult or official.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interjection derived from verb *danken*. Declarative clause has demonstrative subject *das*, copula *ist* in V2, degree adverb *sehr*, predicate adjective *nett*, and preposition *von* governing capitalized formal dative pronoun *Ihnen*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 (responding courteously to answers or assistance) and Schreiben. Essential courtesy marker.

**#13. Vielen Dank!** — Thank you very much!
*Example sentence:* Vielen Dank für Ihre Hilfe. (Thank you very much for your help.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Vielen Dank* (Much thanks [masculine accusative]) + *für* (for [preposition + accusative]) + *Ihre Hilfe* (your help [feminine accusative]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Thank you very much for your help."
  - *Context:* High-register courteous expression commonly concluding letters, emails, or service interactions.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Fixed formula in the Accusative masculine (*Vielen Dank*). Followed by preposition *für* (strictly governs Accusative) + possessive determiner *Ihre* and feminine noun *Hilfe*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Schreiben (formal/semi-formal email opening or closing) and Sprechen Teil 3. Demonstrates higher polite register than simple *Danke*.

**#14. Danke schön!** — Thank you kindly!
*Example sentence:* Danke schön, das Essen war lecker. (Thank you kindly, the food was delicious.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Danke schön* (Thank you kindly) + *das Essen* (the food/meal [neuter]) + *war* (was [past tense of *sein*]) + *lecker* (delicious/tasty).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Thank you kindly, the food was delicious."
  - *Context:* Spoken to a host, cook, or waiter when clearing plates after a good meal.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interjection combined with intensifying particle *schön*. Followed by past tense clause (Präteritum) with subject *das Essen*, copula *war* in V2, and predicate adjective *lecker*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen (restaurants, shops, service dialogues). Warmer and slightly more emphatic than standard *Danke*.

**#15. Bitte schön!** — You're welcome! / Here you go!
*Example sentence:* Hier ist Ihr Kaffee. — Bitte schön! (Here is your coffee. — Here you go!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Hier ist* (Here is) + *Ihr Kaffee* (your coffee [formal possessive masculine]) + *Bitte schön!* (courteous presentation formula: "Here you are!" / "You're welcome!").
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Here is your coffee. — Here you go!"
  - *Context:* The waiter serves the drink and offers the courteous verbal handover formula.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Inversion triggered by local adverb *Hier* in position 1 + copula *ist* in position 2 + subject *Ihr Kaffee* (Nominative masculine). Followed by courteous responsive formula *Bitte schön!*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. Dual function: used when offering/handing over an item ("Here you go") or responding politely to "Danke" ("You're welcome").

**#16. Entschuldigung!** — Excuse me! / Sorry!
*Example sentence:* Entschuldigung, wo ist die Toilette? (Excuse me, where is the toilet?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Entschuldigung* (Excuse me / Apology) + *wo ist* (where is) + *die Toilette* (the restroom/toilet [feminine]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Excuse me, where is the restroom?"
  - *Context:* Indispensable public phrase used in restaurants, train stations, shopping malls, and airports.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Feminine noun (*die Entschuldigung*) used as an exclamation. Second clause is a W-question with question word *wo*, verb *ist* in V2, and feminine subject *die Toilette* (Nominative).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 (approaching strangers to ask for directions or help) and Hören. Standard conversational opener in public.

**#17. Es tut mir leid.** — I'm sorry.
*Example sentence:* Es tut mir leid, ich bin zu spät. (I'm sorry, I'm late.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Es tut mir leid* (Lit. "It does sorrow to-me" -> I am sorry) + *ich* (I) + *bin* (am) + *zu spät* (too late).
  - *Literal translation:* "It does sorrow to me, I am too late."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I'm sorry, I'm running late / I'm late."
  - *Context:* Standard polite apology when arriving past a scheduled time for class, a meeting, or an appointment.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Impersonal idiom: dummy subject *Es*, verb *tut* (3rd person singular of *tun*), dative personal pronoun *mir*, and predicate noun/adverb *leid*. Second clause has subject *ich*, copula *bin* in V2, and predicate *zu spät*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Schreiben (apologizing for absence, missing class, or arriving late) and Sprechen Teil 3. Standard expression of regret.

**#18. Kein Problem!** — No problem!
*Example sentence:* Kein Problem, das kann passieren. (No problem, that can happen.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Kein Problem* (No problem [neuter]) + *das* (that) + *kann* (can [modal *können*]) + *passieren* (happen).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "No problem, that can happen / accidents happen."
  - *Context:* Friendly reassurance offered to someone who has just apologized for a minor mishap or delay.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Negative determiner *Kein* (Nominative/Accusative neuter) modifying noun *Problem*. Second clause has demonstrative subject *das*, modal verb *kann* in V2, and main infinitive *passieren* at the end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. Friendly, informal way to relieve pressure when a partner apologizes or makes a small mistake.

**#19. Wie geht es Ihnen?** — How are you? (formal)
*Example sentence:* Guten Tag, Frau Meier! Wie geht es Ihnen? (Good day, Mrs. Meier! How are you?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Guten Tag* (Good day) + *Frau Meier* (Mrs. Meier) + *Wie* (how) + *geht* (goes) + *es* (it) + *Ihnen* (to you [formal dative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Good day, Mrs. Meier! How goes it to you?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Good day, Mrs. Meier! How are you doing?"
  - *Context:* The canonical formal German greeting and polite inquiry about health and well-being.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Impersonal interrogative clause: question word *Wie*, finite verb *geht* in position 2, dummy pronoun *es*, and capitalized formal personal pronoun *Ihnen* in the Dative case.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen (formal conversation with examiners) and Schreiben (formal/semi-formal opening inquiry). Always capitalize *Ihnen* and remember the dative case.

**#20. Wie geht's?** — How's it going? (informal)
*Example sentence:* Hey, wie geht's? Alles gut? (Hey, how's it going? All good?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Hey* (Hey) + *wie geht's?* (how's it going? [short for *wie geht es*]) + *Alles gut?* (All good? / Everything fine?).
  - *Literal translation:* "Hey, how goes it? Everything good?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Hey, how's it going? Everything all right?"
  - *Context:* Casual greeting between young people, classmates, and close friends.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Contracted colloquial form of *Wie geht es* (*geht's*). Followed by an elliptical informal check-in question with indefinite pronoun *alles* and adjective *gut*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (peer dialogues). Do not use in formal writing or when addressing the exam examiners.

**#21. Mir geht es gut, danke.** — I'm fine, thank you.
*Example sentence:* Wie geht es Ihnen? — Mir geht es gut, danke. (How are you? — I'm fine, thank you.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wie geht es Ihnen?* (How are you? [formal]) + *Mir* (to me [dative]) + *geht* (goes) + *es* (it) + *gut* (well) + *danke* (thanks).
  - *Literal translation:* "How goes it to you? — To me goes it well, thanks."
  - *Natural meaning:* "How are you? — I'm doing well, thank you."
  - *Context:* The standard polite response sequence in German; notice German says "to me it goes well" rather than "I am good".
- **Grammar & Structure:** Dative experiencer construction: dative pronoun *Mir* occupies position 1 (topicalized), finite verb *geht* in V2, grammatical dummy subject *es*, and adverb *gut*. (Never say "Ich bin gut", which translates as "I am morally good / skilled").
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1/2. Critical grammatical point; examiners specifically watch for correct dative pronoun usage (*Mir geht es...*).

**#22. Nicht so gut.** — Not so well.
*Example sentence:* Wie geht's? — Nicht so gut, ich bin krank. (How's it going? — Not so well, I'm sick.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wie geht's?* (How are you?) + *Nicht so gut* (Not so well) + *ich* (I) + *bin* (am) + *krank* (sick/ill).
  - *Literal translation:* "How goes it? — Not so good, I am sick."
  - *Natural meaning:* "How's it going? — Not so well, I'm sick."
  - *Context:* Honest response when dealing with an illness, frequently leading to the sympathetic reply *Gute Besserung!*.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Adverbial negation (*Nicht* + degree modifier *so* + adverb *gut*). Second clause gives the reason: subject *ich*, copula *bin* in V2, and predicate adjective *krank*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen (expressing feelings/states) and Hören (identifying illness or problems in listening dialogues).

**#23. Alles klar!** — All clear! / Got it!
*Example sentence:* Alles klar, wir treffen uns um 10 Uhr. (All clear, we'll meet at 10 o'clock.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Alles klar* (All clear / Understood) + *wir* (we) + *treffen* (meet) + *uns* (ourselves [reflexive]) + *um 10 Uhr* (at 10 o'clock).
  - *Literal translation:* "All clear, we meet ourselves at 10 clock."
  - *Natural meaning:* "All right, we will meet at 10:00 AM."
  - *Context:* Confirming a scheduled rendezvous or plan with a peer or colleague.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Idiomatic phrase with indefinite pronoun *alles* and adjective *klar*. Second clause features reflexive verb *treffen*, subject *wir*, reflexive pronoun *uns* (Accusative), and prepositional time phrase *um 10 Uhr* (Accusative).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 (agreeing on meeting times or confirming plans) and Hören. Signals complete understanding.

**#24. Herzlich willkommen!** — Welcome!
*Example sentence:* Herzlich willkommen in Deutschland! (Welcome to Germany!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Herzlich* (cordially / from the heart) + *willkommen* (welcome) + *in Deutschland* (in Germany).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "A warm welcome to Germany!"
  - *Context:* Welcoming arriving visitors, immigrants, or tourists at border points, airports, or language institutions.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Adverb *herzlich* (cordially) + adjective/participle *willkommen*. Followed by directional/locational preposition *in* governing neuter country noun without article (*Deutschland*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Hören (announcements at train stations, airports, hotels) and Leseverstehen (signs and welcoming notices).

## Introducing Yourself and Others

**#25. Ich heiße…** — My name is…
*Example sentence:* Ich heiße Anna. Und du? (My name is Anna. And you?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich* (I) + *heiße* (am named / am called) + *Anna* (Anna) + *Und du?* (And you? [informal nominative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "I am called Anna. And you?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "My name is Anna. And what about you?"
  - *Context:* Informal peer self-introduction, inviting the conversation partner to introduce themselves.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject pronoun *Ich* (Nominative) + regular stem-conjugated verb *heißen* in 1st person singular (*heiße*, V2). Elliptical counter-question *Und du?* with Nominative 2nd person pronoun *du*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (mandatory self-introduction / Vorstellung: Name, Alter, Land, Wohnort, Sprachen, Beruf, Hobby).

**#26. Mein Name ist…** — My name is…
*Example sentence:* Mein Name ist Thomas Weber. (My name is Thomas Weber.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Mein Name* (My name [masculine]) + *ist* (is) + *Thomas Weber* (Thomas Weber).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "My name is Thomas Weber."
  - *Context:* Formal or professional self-introduction, giving both first and last name clearly.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Possessive determiner *Mein* (Nominative masculine) modifying subject *Name*, copula *ist* in V2, and predicate noun *Thomas Weber*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (standard formal alternative to *Ich heiße...*) and Schreiben (formal self-identification in application or registration forms).

**#27. Wie heißen Sie?** — What is your name? (formal)
*Example sentence:* Guten Tag! Wie heißen Sie? (Good day! What is your name?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Guten Tag* (Good day) + *Wie* (how) + *heißen* (are named) + *Sie* (you [formal nominative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Good day! How are you called?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Hello! What is your name?"
  - *Context:* Formal inquiry used by officials, receptionists, or speaking test examiners when asking for someone's identity.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative W-question: interrogative adverb *Wie*, finite verb *heißen* in V2, and capitalized formal subject pronoun *Sie* (Nominative).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (asking your partner questions based on prompt cards / Thema: Name/Person).

**#28. Wie heißt du?** — What is your name? (informal)
*Example sentence:* Hallo! Wie heißt du? — Ich heiße Max. (Hello! What's your name? — My name is Max.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Hallo!* (Hello!) + *Wie heißt du?* (How are you called? [informal]) + *Ich heiße Max* (I am called Max).
  - *Literal translation:* "Hello! How are you called? — I am called Max."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Hi! What's your name? — My name is Max."
  - *Context:* Casual introduction between children, university students, or sports teammates.
- **Grammar & Structure:** W-question: *Wie* + 2nd person singular verb *heißt* (stem *heiß-* + suffix *-t* because the stem ends in *-ß*) + informal subject pronoun *du*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2. Note conjugation rule: verbs whose stems end in *-s, -ß, -z* take only *-t* for *du* rather than *-st*.

**#29. Woher kommen Sie?** — Where are you from? (formal)
*Example sentence:* Woher kommen Sie? — Ich komme aus Japan. (Where are you from? — I'm from Japan.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Woher* (Where from) + *kommen Sie?* (come you? [formal]) + *Ich komme* (I come) + *aus Japan* (from Japan).
  - *Literal translation:* "Where-from come you? — I come out-of Japan."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Where are you from? — I come from Japan."
  - *Context:* Benchmark question in Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 testing country of origin and preposition *aus*.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative compound adverb *Woher* (origin/direction from), finite verb *kommen* in V2, subject *Sie*. Answer uses preposition *aus* (+ Dative: *aus Japan*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (standard examiner prompt: "Woher kommen Sie?") and Teil 2. Preposition *aus* is mandatory for geographic origin.

**#30. Woher kommst du?** — Where are you from? (informal)
*Example sentence:* Woher kommst du? — Ich komme aus der Türkei. (Where are you from? — I come from Turkey.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Woher kommst du?* (Where do you come from? [informal]) + *Ich komme* (I come) + *aus der Türkei* (from the Turkey [dative feminine]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Where-from comest thou? — I come out-of the Turkey."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Where are you from? — I'm from Turkey."
  - *Context:* Illustrates the mandatory dative feminine article *der* used when naming countries that possess grammatical gender.
- **Grammar & Structure:** *Woher* + 2nd person singular verb *kommst* in V2 + subject *du*. Note: countries with grammatical gender require the dative article after *aus* (*die Türkei* -> *aus der Türkei*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 & 2. High-frequency scoring trap: countries with feminine, masculine, or plural articles (die Schweiz, die Türkei, die USA, der Irak) must include the declined article.

**#31. Ich komme aus…** — I come from…
*Example sentence:* Ich komme aus Brasilien und lebe jetzt in Berlin. (I come from Brazil and now live in Berlin.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich komme* (I come) + *aus Brasilien* (from Brazil) + *und* (and) + *lebe jetzt* (live now) + *in Berlin* (in Berlin).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I come from Brazil and now live in Berlin."
  - *Context:* Dual statement combining origin (*Herkunft*) and current residence (*Wohnort*) in the oral exam presentation.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Compound sentence coordinated by conjunction *und* (position 0). First clause: *Ich* + *komme* (V2) + *aus Brasilien*. Second clause: verb *lebe* in V2 + temporal adverb *jetzt* + locative prepositional phrase *in Berlin* (Dative).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (stating country of origin during the oral self-introduction).

**#32. Ich wohne in…** — I live in…
*Example sentence:* Ich wohne in München seit zwei Jahren. (I have lived in Munich for two years.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich wohne* (I live/reside) + *in München* (in Munich) + *seit* (since / for [preposition + dative]) + *zwei Jahren* (two years [dative plural]).
  - *Literal translation:* "I live in Munich since two years."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I have lived in Munich for two years."
  - *Context:* Expressing duration of stay using present tense + *seit* (unlike English, which requires present perfect continuous).
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + verb *wohne* (1st person singular, V2) + locative preposition *in* + proper city noun *München*. Preposition *seit* strictly governs Dative plural (*zwei Jahren* takes plural dative suffix *-n*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (Wohnort). Notice present tense used with *seit* for ongoing actions that started in the past.

**#33. Ich bin … Jahre alt.** — I am … years old.
*Example sentence:* Ich bin 25 Jahre alt. (I am 25 years old.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich bin* (I am) + *25 Jahre* (25 years [neuter plural]) + *alt* (old).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I am 25 years old."
  - *Context:* Answering the examiner prompt "Alter" (age) in Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + irregular copula *bin* (1st person singular of *sein*, V2) + measure phrase *25 Jahre alt* (neuter plural *die Jahre*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (Alter / age). Examiners often ask follow-up questions testing numerical comprehension and pronunciation.

**#34. Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch.** — I speak a little German.
*Example sentence:* Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch, aber nicht so gut. (I speak a little German, but not so well.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich spreche* (I speak) + *ein bisschen* (a little bit) + *Deutsch* (German) + *aber* (but) + *nicht so gut* (not so well).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I speak a little German, but not so well."
  - *Context:* Self-assessment of language skills ("Welche Sprachen sprechen Sie?") showing modesty and conversational fluency.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Irregular stem-changing verb *sprechen* in 1st person singular (*spreche*, regular stem in 1st person, V2) + indefinite quantifier *ein bisschen* + direct object *Deutsch*. Coordinated by *aber* (position 0).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (Sprachen). Polite, realistic, and highly natural response when presenting your language abilities.

**#35. Ich lerne Deutsch.** — I am learning German.
*Example sentence:* Ich lerne Deutsch seit drei Monaten. (I have been learning German for three months.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich lerne* (I learn / study) + *Deutsch* (German) + *seit* (since / for) + *drei Monaten* (three months [dative plural]).
  - *Literal translation:* "I learn German since three months."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I have been learning German for three months."
  - *Context:* Stating study background with *seit* + dative plural noun *Monaten*.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Regular verb *lernen* in 1st person singular (*lerne*, V2) + direct object *Deutsch*. Preposition *seit* + dative plural noun *Monaten* (masculine *der Monat* -> plural *Monate* -> dative plural *Monaten*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 and Schreiben. Demonstrates solid control of *seit* + Dative to express ongoing duration.

**#36. Freut mich!** — Pleased to meet you!
*Example sentence:* Mein Name ist Petra. — Freut mich! (My name is Petra. — Pleased to meet you!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Mein Name ist Petra* (My name is Petra) + *Freut mich!* (Pleases me! [short for *Es freut mich*]).
  - *Literal translation:* "My name is Petra. — It pleases me!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "My name is Petra. — Nice to meet you!"
  - *Context:* Polite conversational response when being introduced to a stranger or testing peer.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Ellipsis of the impersonal reflexive clause *[Es] freut mich* (verb *freuen* in 3rd person singular + 1st person singular accusative personal pronoun *mich*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (initial conversational opening when introduced to your testing partner).

**#37. Das ist mein Freund / meine Freundin.** — This is my friend.
*Example sentence:* Das ist mein Freund Marco, er kommt aus Italien. (This is my friend Marco, he comes from Italy.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Das ist* (This is) + *mein Freund Marco* (my friend Marco [masculine]) + *er kommt* (he comes) + *aus Italien* (from Italy).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "This is my friend Marco, he comes from Italy."
  - *Context:* Introducing a third party, stating their name and geographic origin.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Demonstrative pronoun *Das* + copula *ist* (V2) + predicate noun phrase with possessive pronoun (*mein Freund* masc. Nom. / *meine Freundin* fem. Nom. with *-e* suffix). Second clause has masculine pronoun *er* + V2 verb *kommt*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (talking about family, friends, and acquaintances) and Schreiben (writing about social plans).

**#38. Ich bin verheiratet / ledig.** — I am married / single.
*Example sentence:* Ich bin verheiratet und habe zwei Kinder. (I am married and have two children.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich bin* (I am) + *verheiratet* (married) + *und* (and) + *habe* (have) + *zwei Kinder* (two children [neuter plural]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I am married and have two children."
  - *Context:* Speaking about family status (*Familienstand*) during self-introduction or social conversations.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + copula *bin* in V2 + predicate adjective *verheiratet* (participle used as adjective) or *ledig*. Coordinated by *und* with transitive verb *habe* in V2 + Accusative plural object *zwei Kinder*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (Familienstand / marital status). Frequent examiner question during the introductory phase.

---

## Asking for and Giving Directions

**#39. Wo ist…?** — Where is…?
*Example sentence:* Wo ist der Bahnhof, bitte? (Where is the train station, please?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wo ist* (Where is) + *der Bahnhof* (the train station [masculine nominative]) + *bitte* (please).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Where is the train station, please?"
  - *Context:* Asking directions to public transit hubs on city flashcards.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative adverb *Wo* (location) + copula *ist* in V2 + subject *der Bahnhof* (Nominative masculine) + politeness particle *bitte*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 (asking for locations based on prompt cards showing public buildings like "Bahnhof", "Bank", "Post").

**#40. Wie komme ich zum / zur…?** — How do I get to the…?
*Example sentence:* Wie komme ich zum Krankenhaus? (How do I get to the hospital?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wie komme ich* (How come/get I) + *zum* (*zu* + *dem* = to the [dative neuter]) + *Krankenhaus* (hospital).
  - *Literal translation:* "How come I to-the hospital?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "How do I get to the hospital?"
  - *Context:* Common wayfinding inquiry asking for the route to a medical facility.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative adverb *Wie* + finite verb *komme* in V2 + subject pronoun *ich*. Contraction *zum* = *zu* + *dem* (Dative neuter: *das Krankenhaus* -> *dem Krankenhaus*). Note: *zur* = *zu* + *der* for feminine destinations.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 & 3. Highly idiomatic structure for asking directions in everyday city navigation tasks.

**#41. Gehen Sie geradeaus.** — Go straight ahead.
*Example sentence:* Gehen Sie geradeaus und dann links. (Go straight ahead and then left.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Gehen Sie* (Go you [formal imperative]) + *geradeaus* (straight ahead) + *und* (and) + *dann* (then) + *links* (left).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Go straight ahead and then left."
  - *Context:* Providing clear street navigation instructions.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Formal imperative: finite verb *Gehen* in position 1 + formal subject pronoun *Sie* + directional adverb *geradeaus*. Second clause coordinated by *und* with temporal/directional adverbs *dann links*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 (giving directions / Wegbeschreibung) and Hören (listening to route descriptions).

**#42. Biegen Sie links ab.** — Turn left.
*Example sentence:* Biegen Sie links ab, dann sehen Sie die Apotheke. (Turn left, then you'll see the pharmacy.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Biegen Sie ... ab* (Turn you [formal imperative of *abbiegen*]) + *links* (left) + *dann* (then) + *sehen Sie* (see you [inverted verb-subject]) + *die Apotheke* (the pharmacy [feminine accusative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Turn you left off, then see you the pharmacy."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Turn left, then you will see the pharmacy."
  - *Context:* Multi-step navigation guiding someone to a local landmark.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Separable verb *abbiegen* in formal imperative: stem *Biegen* in position 1 + *Sie* + directional adverb *links* + separable prefix *ab* placed at clause end. Second clause begins with adverb *dann* in position 1 triggering subject-verb inversion (*sehen Sie*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 and Hören. A key benchmark for separable verb mechanics (*ab-biegen*).

**#43. Biegen Sie rechts ab.** — Turn right.
*Example sentence:* Biegen Sie an der Ampel rechts ab. (Turn right at the traffic light.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Biegen Sie ... ab* (Turn you [formal imperative]) + *an der Ampel* (at the traffic light [dative feminine]) + *rechts* (right).
  - *Literal translation:* "Turn you at the traffic-light right off."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Turn right at the traffic light."
  - *Context:* Directing someone using street infrastructure as a spatial reference point.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Formal imperative of separable verb *abbiegen*. Two-way preposition *an* + Dative feminine noun phrase *der Ampel* (*die Ampel* -> *der Ampel* indicating static location of the turning point) + prefix *ab* at the end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 & Hören. Pairs with *links abbiegen*; *die Ampel* (traffic light) is high-frequency exam vocabulary.

**#44. Es ist in der Nähe.** — It is nearby.
*Example sentence:* Der Supermarkt ist in der Nähe, nur fünf Minuten zu Fuß. (The supermarket is nearby, only five minutes on foot.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Der Supermarkt* (The supermarket) + *ist* (is) + *in der Nähe* (in the vicinity / nearby [dative feminine]) + *nur* (only) + *fünf Minuten* (five minutes) + *zu Fuß* (on foot).
  - *Literal translation:* "The supermarket is in the vicinity, only five minutes to foot."
  - *Natural meaning:* "The supermarket is nearby, only a five-minute walk."
  - *Context:* Explaining proximity and walking distance to daily services.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Der Supermarkt* + copula *ist* in V2 + fixed prepositional phrase *in der Nähe* (two-way preposition *in* + Dative feminine noun *der Nähe*). Followed by fixed idiom *zu Fuß* (on foot).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen & Hören. Appears frequently in dialogues estimating walking distances to amenities.

**#45. Es ist weit weg.** — It is far away.
*Example sentence:* Der Flughafen ist weit weg, nehmen Sie den Bus. (The airport is far away, take the bus.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Der Flughafen* (The airport) + *ist* (is) + *weit weg* (far away) + *nehmen Sie* (take you [formal imperative]) + *den Bus* (the bus [masculine accusative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "The airport is far away, take you the bus."
  - *Natural meaning:* "The airport is far away, take the bus."
  - *Context:* Recommending public transit for distant destinations.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Der Flughafen* + copula *ist* in V2 + adverbial phrase *weit weg*. Followed by an imperative clause: irregular verb *nehmen* in formal imperative (*Nehmen Sie*) + direct object in Accusative masculine (*den Bus*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 and Hören (identifying appropriate public transportation options based on distance).

**#46. Können Sie mir helfen?** — Can you help me?
*Example sentence:* Entschuldigung, können Sie mir helfen? Ich bin verloren. (Excuse me, can you help me? I am lost.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Entschuldigung* (Excuse me) + *können Sie* (can you [formal]) + *mir* (me [dative]) + *helfen?* (help?) + *Ich bin* (I am) + *verloren* (lost).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Excuse me, can you help me? I am lost."
  - *Context:* Asking strangers for assistance when disoriented in a new city.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Modal verb Yes/No question: modal auxiliary *Können* in position 1, formal subject *Sie*, dative personal pronoun *mir* (governed by dative verb *helfen*), and main infinitive *helfen* at clause end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. Essential for formulating polite requests for assistance. Note that *helfen* strictly takes the Dative case (*mir*, *Ihnen*).

**#47. Ich suche…** — I'm looking for…
*Example sentence:* Ich suche die Bibliothek. Ist sie hier in der Nähe? (I'm looking for the library. Is it nearby?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich suche* (I seek / am looking for) + *die Bibliothek* (the library [feminine]) + *Ist sie* (Is it [referring to *die Bibliothek*]) + *hier in der Nähe?* (here nearby?).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I'm looking for the library. Is it nearby?"
  - *Context:* Inquiry about local educational or municipal facilities.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + regular transitive verb *suche* in V2 + direct object in Accusative feminine (*die Bibliothek*). Second clause uses 3rd person feminine pronoun *sie* referencing the library.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 (initiating inquiries when given prompt cards depicting city infrastructure).

**#48. Gibt es hier in der Nähe einen / eine / ein…?** — Is there a … nearby?
*Example sentence:* Gibt es hier in der Nähe eine Bank? (Is there a bank nearby?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Gibt es* (Gives it -> Is there) + *hier in der Nähe* (here in the vicinity) + *eine Bank?* (a bank? [feminine accusative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "Gives it here in the vicinity a bank?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Is there a bank nearby?"
  - *Context:* Standard question using *Gibt es* + accusative to check for facilities.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Impersonal expression *es gibt* inverted into question form: verb *Gibt* in position 1 + dummy subject *es*. *Es gibt* strictly governs the Accusative case (*einen* masc. / *eine* fem. / *ein* neut.).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. One of the most frequently assessed structures; candidates must remember to decline following nouns in the Accusative case.

**#49. Auf der linken / rechten Seite.** — On the left / right side.
*Example sentence:* Das Hotel ist auf der rechten Seite. (The hotel is on the right side.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Das Hotel* (The hotel [neuter]) + *ist* (is) + *auf der rechten Seite* (on the right side [dative feminine]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "The hotel is on the right-hand side."
  - *Context:* Indicating orientation along a street.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Das Hotel* (Nominative neuter) + copula *ist* in V2 + two-way preposition *auf* + Dative feminine noun phrase (*der rechten Seite*, weak adjective ending *-en*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Hören & Sprechen Teil 3. Standard phrase for pinpointing the exact side of the road an entrance or building is situated on.

**#50. An der Ecke.** — On the corner.
*Example sentence:* Die Bäckerei ist an der Ecke. (The bakery is on the corner.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Die Bäckerei* (The bakery [feminine]) + *ist* (is) + *an der Ecke* (at the corner [dative feminine]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "The bakery is on the corner."
  - *Context:* Locating shops at street intersections.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Die Bäckerei* + copula *ist* in V2 + two-way preposition *an* + Dative feminine noun phrase *der Ecke* (static location).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 & Hören. Essential landmark description for neighborhood maps and orientation tasks.

---

## Shopping and Prices

**#51. Was kostet das?** — How much does that cost?
*Example sentence:* Entschuldigung, was kostet das T-Shirt? (Excuse me, how much does the T-shirt cost?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Entschuldigung* (Excuse me) + *was kostet* (what costs / how much is) + *das T-Shirt?* (the T-shirt? [neuter]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Excuse me, how much does the T-shirt cost?"
  - *Context:* Direct price inquiry when browsing clothing racks.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative W-question: interrogative pronoun *Was* + finite verb *kostet* in V2 + subject *das T-Shirt* (Nominative neuter).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (Thema: Einkaufen / shopping cards) and Teil 3 (asking prices in roleplay scenarios).

**#52. Wie viel kostet…?** — How much does … cost?
*Example sentence:* Wie viel kostet ein Kilo Äpfel? (How much does a kilo of apples cost?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wie viel kostet* (How much costs) + *ein Kilo Äpfel?* (one kilo [of] apples? [neuter singular unit]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "How much does a kilogram of apples cost?"
  - *Context:* Purchasing produce by weight at a weekly farmers' market (*Wochenmarkt*) or grocery store.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative phrase *Wie viel* + verb *kostet* in V2 + singular subject *ein Kilo Äpfel*. Note: if the subject is plural without a unit, the verb becomes plural (*Wie viel kosten die Äpfel?*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 & Hören. Pay close attention to subject-verb agreement: singular *kostet* vs. plural *kosten*.

**#53. Das ist zu teuer.** — That is too expensive.
*Example sentence:* 50 Euro? Das ist zu teuer! (50 euros? That is too expensive!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *50 Euro?* (50 euros?) + *Das ist* (That is) + *zu teuer!* (too expensive!).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "50 euros? That is too expensive!"
  - *Context:* Declining a purchase based on price during shopping roleplays.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Demonstrative subject *Das* + copula *ist* in V2 + degree adverb *zu* (indicating excess) + predicate adjective *teuer*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (bargaining / reacting to price information) and Hören (identifying why a speaker declines a purchase).

**#54. Das ist günstig / billig.** — That is cheap / affordable.
*Example sentence:* Nur zwei Euro? Das ist günstig! (Only two euros? That's affordable!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Nur zwei Euro?* (Only two euros?) + *Das ist* (That is) + *günstig!* (affordable / reasonable / a good deal!).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Only two euros? That's affordable!"
  - *Context:* Expressing satisfaction with an economical price.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Restrictive particle *Nur* + nominal currency phrase. Main clause: subject *Das* + copula *ist* in V2 + predicate adjective *günstig* (positive value connotation) or *billig* (low price).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 & Hören. German speakers prefer *günstig* or *preiswert* ("good value") over *billig*, which can imply inferior quality.

**#55. Ich möchte… kaufen. / Ich möchte…** — I would like to buy… / I would like…
*Example sentence:* Ich möchte ein Buch kaufen. (I would like to buy a book.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich möchte* (I would like) + *ein Buch* (a book [neuter accusative]) + *kaufen* (to buy).
  - *Literal translation:* "I would-like a book to-buy."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I would like to buy a book."
  - *Context:* Stating purchase intentions upon entering a bookstore or retail shop.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Polite Konjunktiv II form of *mögen* functioning as a modal verb: *Ich möchte* in V2 + direct object in Accusative neuter (*ein Buch*) + infinitive *kaufen* at the end of the sentence bracket.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 and Schreiben (inquiries about merchandise). Standard polite formulation for expressing purchasing intentions.

**#56. Haben Sie…?** — Do you have…?
*Example sentence:* Haben Sie das in einer anderen Farbe? (Do you have that in a different colour?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Haben Sie* (Have you [formal]) + *das* (that [accusative]) + *in einer anderen Farbe?* (in another colour? [dative feminine]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Do you have this in a different colour?"
  - *Context:* Inquiring about item variations with a store clerk.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Yes/No question: finite verb *Haben* in position 1 + formal subject pronoun *Sie* + direct object *das* (Accusative). Preposition *in* takes Dative for state/appearance (*einer anderen Farbe*, feminine Dative).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 & 3. Universal customer opening question when inquiring about stock, alternatives, or sizes.

**#57. Ich nehme das.** — I'll take that.
*Example sentence:* Das Kleid gefällt mir. Ich nehme das. (I like the dress. I'll take it.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Das Kleid* (The dress [neuter]) + *gefällt mir* (pleases to-me [dative]) + *Ich nehme das* (I take that [accusative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "The dress pleases to-me. I take that."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I like the dress. I'll take it."
  - *Context:* Deciding to purchase an article of clothing.
- **Grammar & Structure:** First clause: subject *Das Kleid* + dative verb *gefällt* (stem-vowel change *a* -> *ä*) + dative pronoun *mir*. Second clause: subject *Ich* + verb *nehme* in V2 (irregular verb *nehmen*) + accusative direct object *das*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. Decisive purchasing phrase concluding shopping dialogues. Note irregular conjugation of *nehmen* (*du nimmst*, *er nimmt*).

**#58. Kann ich mit Karte bezahlen?** — Can I pay by card?
*Example sentence:* Kann ich mit Karte bezahlen oder nur bar? (Can I pay by card or only cash?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Kann ich* (Can I) + *mit Karte bezahlen* (pay by card [dative feminine]) + *oder* (or) + *nur bar?* (only cash?).
  - *Literal translation:* "Can I with card pay or only cash?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Can I pay by card, or is it cash only?"
  - *Context:* Clarifying accepted payment methods at checkout counters in Germany, where cash is still widely used.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Modal Yes/No question: auxiliary *Kann* in position 1 + subject *ich* + prepositional phrase *mit Karte* (preposition *mit* strictly governs Dative) + main infinitive *bezahlen* at the end. Disjunctive conjunction *oder* connects adverbial modifier *nur bar*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 and Hören. Critical practical question for payment procedures in shops, supermarkets, and restaurants.

**#59. Ich brauche eine Tüte, bitte.** — I need a bag, please.
*Example sentence:* Ich brauche eine Tüte, bitte. — Das kostet 10 Cent. (I need a bag, please. — That costs 10 cents.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich brauche* (I need) + *eine Tüte* (a bag [feminine accusative]) + *bitte* (please) + *Das kostet 10 Cent* (That costs 10 cents).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I need a bag, please. — That costs 10 cents."
  - *Context:* Supermarket checkout transaction, where carrier bags incur a nominal fee by law.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + regular transitive verb *brauche* in V2 + direct object in Accusative feminine (*eine Tüte*) + politeness marker *bitte*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. Common exam card trigger (picture of a plastic/paper bag at checkout).

**#60. Wo kann ich das umtauschen?** — Where can I exchange this?
*Example sentence:* Das passt nicht. Wo kann ich das umtauschen? (It doesn't fit. Where can I exchange this?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Das passt nicht* (That fits not / That doesn't fit) + *Wo kann ich* (Where can I) + *das* (that) + *umtauschen?* (exchange? [separable verb]).
  - *Literal translation:* "That fits not. Where can I that exchange?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "This doesn't fit. Where can I exchange it?"
  - *Context:* Customer service inquiry regarding exchanges and returns.
- **Grammar & Structure:** First clause: subject *Das* + verb *passt* in V2 + negative particle *nicht*. Second clause: W-question with *Wo*, modal verb *kann* in V2, subject *ich*, object *das*, and separable verb infinitive *umtauschen* at clause end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 and Hören. Key customer service vocabulary item dealing with post-purchase returns.

**#61. Haben Sie das in Größe…?** — Do you have that in size…?
*Example sentence:* Haben Sie das in Größe M? (Do you have that in size M?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Haben Sie* (Have you [formal]) + *das* (that) + *in Größe M?* (in size M? [dative feminine]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Do you have this in size M?"
  - *Context:* Requesting a specific clothing size from a sales assistant.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Verb-first question: *Haben* (V1) + formal subject *Sie* + direct object *das* (Accusative) + prepositional phrase *in Größe M* (Dative feminine).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 (clothing store dialogues). Always capitalize the feminine noun *die Größe*.

**#62. Der Laden macht um … Uhr auf / zu.** — The shop opens / closes at … o'clock.
*Example sentence:* Der Laden macht um 9 Uhr auf und um 20 Uhr zu. (The shop opens at 9 and closes at 8 pm.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Der Laden* (The shop [masculine]) + *macht ... auf* (opens [separable *aufmachen*]) + *um 9 Uhr* (at 9 o'clock) + *und* (and) + *um 20 Uhr* (at 8 pm / 20:00) + *zu* (closes [separable *zumachen*]).
  - *Literal translation:* "The shop makes at 9 clock open and at 20 clock closed."
  - *Natural meaning:* "The shop opens at 9:00 AM and closes at 8:00 PM."
  - *Context:* Understanding store operating hours (*Öffnungszeiten*) on signs or over the phone.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Separable verbs *aufmachen* (to open) and *zumachen* (to close): subject *Der Laden* (masculine Nominative) + finite verb stem *macht* in V2 + time phrase *um 9 Uhr* + prefix *auf* at the end; coordinated with *und* and prefix *zu*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Leseverstehen (interpreting opening hour notices / Öffnungszeiten) and Hören (store announcements).

---

## At the Restaurant / Café

**#63. Ich hätte gern…** — I would like… (polite)
*Example sentence:* Ich hätte gern eine Tasse Kaffee, bitte. (I would like a cup of coffee, please.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich hätte gern* (I would gladly have / I'd like) + *eine Tasse Kaffee* (a cup [of] coffee [feminine accusative]) + *bitte* (please).
  - *Literal translation:* "I would-have gladly a cup coffee, please."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I would like a cup of coffee, please."
  - *Context:* Polite standard phrasing for ordering in cafés and bakeries.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Polite Konjunktiv II form of auxiliary *haben*: *Ich hätte* in V2 + adverbial particle *gern* + direct object in Accusative feminine (*eine Tasse Kaffee*) + politeness marker *bitte*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. Far more polite than *Ich will* (which is perceived as abrupt or rude in German service interactions).

**#64. Die Rechnung, bitte.** — The bill, please.
*Example sentence:* Wir möchten zahlen. Die Rechnung, bitte. (We would like to pay. The bill, please.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wir möchten* (We would like) + *zahlen* (to pay) + *Die Rechnung* (The bill/check [feminine]) + *bitte* (please).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "We would like to pay. The bill, please."
  - *Context:* Requesting the check at the end of a restaurant meal.
- **Grammar & Structure:** First clause: subject *Wir* + modal *möchten* in V2 + infinitive *zahlen* at clause end. Second clause is an elliptical accusative noun phrase: *Die Rechnung* (feminine object of unstated *Bringen Sie bitte...*) + *bitte*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. The universal, polite formula for asking for the check at any dining establishment.

**#65. Zum Mitnehmen, bitte.** — To go, please.
*Example sentence:* Einen Kaffee, bitte. Zum Mitnehmen. (A coffee, please. To go.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Einen Kaffee* (A coffee [masculine accusative: *[Ich möchte] einen Kaffee*]) + *bitte* (please) + *Zum Mitnehmen* (to-the take-with -> to go / takeaway).
  - *Literal translation:* "A coffee, please. To the taking-along."
  - *Natural meaning:* "A coffee, please. To go."
  - *Context:* Ordering takeout at a beverage counter or café window.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Prepositional phrase: contraction *zum* (*zu* + *dem*, Dative neuter) governing nominalized infinitive *Mitnehmen* (capitalized neuter noun).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 & Hören. Essential for ordering takeout beverages and snacks at bakeries, kiosks, and snack bars (Imbiss).

**#66. Zum Hier-Essen, bitte.** — For here, please.
*Example sentence:* Zwei Brötchen, bitte. Zum Hier-Essen. (Two rolls, please. For here.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Zwei Brötchen* (Two bread rolls [neuter plural]) + *bitte* (please) + *Zum Hier-Essen* (to-the here-eating -> for here / dine in).
  - *Literal translation:* "Two rolls, please. To the here-eating."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Two bread rolls, please. For here."
  - *Context:* Clarifying dine-in consumption at a bakery café.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Prepositional phrase: *zum* (*zu dem*, Dative) governing nominalized compound infinitive *Hier-Essen* (neuter noun, capitalized).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. Direct counterpart to *Zum Mitnehmen*. Standard response when the cashier asks "Hier essen oder mitnehmen?".

**#67. Haben Sie eine Speisekarte?** — Do you have a menu?
*Example sentence:* Haben Sie eine Speisekarte auf Englisch? (Do you have a menu in English?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Haben Sie* (Have you [formal]) + *eine Speisekarte* (a menu [feminine accusative]) + *auf Englisch?* (in English?).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Do you have an English menu?"
  - *Context:* Requesting an English menu version in an international restaurant.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Verb-first Yes/No question: *Haben* in position 1 + formal subject *Sie* + direct object in Accusative feminine (*eine Speisekarte*) + prepositional modifier *auf Englisch*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3 and Hören. False friend alert: *die Speisekarte* is the menu booklet; in German, *das Menü* refers to a fixed multi-course set meal.

**#68. Ich bin Vegetarier / Vegetarierin.** — I am a vegetarian.
*Example sentence:* Ich bin Vegetarierin. Was können Sie empfehlen? (I am a vegetarian (f). What can you recommend?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich bin* (I am) + *Vegetarierin* (vegetarian [female noun without article]) + *Was können Sie* (What can you [formal]) + *empfehlen?* (recommend?).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "I am a vegetarian. What can you recommend?"
  - *Context:* Inquiring about plant-based options from the waitstaff.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + copula *bin* in V2 + zero-article predicate noun (*Vegetarier* masculine / *Vegetarierin* feminine with gender suffix *-in*). Second clause: W-question with modal verb *können* in V2 and infinitive *empfehlen* at the end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. Note that in German, dietary identities and professions omit the indefinite article (*Ich bin Vegetarier*, not *Ich bin ein Vegetarier*).

**#69. Was empfehlen Sie?** — What do you recommend?
*Example sentence:* Das ist mein erstes Mal hier. Was empfehlen Sie? (This is my first time here. What do you recommend?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Das ist* (That is) + *mein erstes Mal* (my first time [neuter]) + *hier* (here) + *Was empfehlen Sie?* (What do you recommend?).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "This is my first time here. What do you recommend?"
  - *Context:* Asking the server for house specialties and recommendations.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative W-question: question pronoun *Was* + irregular verb *empfehlen* in V2 (stem-changing verb *e* -> *ie* in 2nd/3rd person singular) + formal subject *Sie*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. Extremely natural conversational turn when interacting with waiters during oral exam roleplays.

**#70. Ist der Tisch frei?** — Is this table free?
*Example sentence:* Entschuldigung, ist der Tisch frei? (Excuse me, is this table free?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Entschuldigung* (Excuse me) + *ist* (is) + *der Tisch* (the table [masculine]) + *frei?* (free / available?).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Excuse me, is this table free?"
  - *Context:* Asking whether a table or seat is vacant in an eatery or beer garden.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Verb-first question: copula *ist* in position 1 + subject *der Tisch* (Nominative masculine) + predicate adjective *frei*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. Essential etiquette in German café and beer garden culture, where sharing tables with strangers is common practice.

**#71. Einen Tisch für zwei Personen, bitte.** — A table for two, please.
*Example sentence:* Guten Abend! Einen Tisch für zwei Personen, bitte. (Good evening! A table for two, please.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Guten Abend!* (Good evening!) + *Einen Tisch* (A table [masculine accusative: *[Wir möchten] einen Tisch*]) + *für zwei Personen* (for two persons [accusative feminine plural]) + *bitte* (please).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Good evening! A table for two people, please."
  - *Context:* Requesting seating upon arrival at a restaurant.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Elliptical accusative object phrase: *Einen Tisch* (Accusative masculine: *[Ich möchte] einen Tisch*) + preposition *für* (strictly governs Accusative: *zwei Personen*, feminine plural) + *bitte*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. Highlighted by examiners to verify that masculine direct objects use the declined article *einen*.

**#72. Das schmeckt sehr gut!** — That tastes very good!
*Example sentence:* Das Schnitzel schmeckt sehr gut! (The schnitzel tastes very good!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Das Schnitzel* (The schnitzel [neuter]) + *schmeckt* (tastes) + *sehr gut!* (very good / delicious!).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "The schnitzel tastes delicious!"
  - *Context:* Giving positive feedback to the server when they ask how the food is.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Das Schnitzel* (Nominative neuter) + verb *schmeckt* in V2 + degree adverb *sehr* + adverb *gut*. (To indicate the person enjoying it, use a dative object: *Das schmeckt mir gut*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3. *Schmecken* is strictly reserved for gustatory taste in German; never use *fühlen* or *kosten* in this context.

**#73. Ich habe eine Allergie gegen…** — I have an allergy to…
*Example sentence:* Ich habe eine Allergie gegen Nüsse. (I have an allergy to nuts.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich habe* (I have) + *eine Allergie* (an allergy [feminine accusative]) + *gegen Nüsse* (against / to nuts [accusative plural]).
  - *Literal translation:* "I have an allergy against nuts."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I have a nut allergy."
  - *Context:* Communicating dietary restrictions to kitchen staff or food servers.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + finite verb *habe* in V2 + direct object in Accusative feminine (*eine Allergie*) + preposition *gegen* (governs Accusative plural: *Nüsse*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 and Schreiben. Crucial real-life safety phrase; note that *gegen* always governs the Accusative case.

**#74. Ein Glas Wasser, bitte.** — A glass of water, please.
*Example sentence:* Kann ich ein Glas Wasser bekommen, bitte? (Can I get a glass of water, please?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Kann ich* (Can I) + *ein Glas Wasser* (a glass [of] water [neuter accusative]) + *bekommen* (receive / get) + *bitte?* (please?).
  - *Literal translation:* "Can I a glass water get, please?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "Could I get a glass of water, please?"
  - *Context:* Ordering table water politely with the modal verb *können*.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Modal question: *Kann* in position 1 + subject *ich* + direct object in Accusative neuter (*ein Glas Wasser*, appositive noun phrase without preposition) + main infinitive *bekommen* at clause end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 3. Note German syntax: nouns of quantity directly precede contents without a connecting preposition (*ein Glas Wasser*, *eine Tasse Kaffee*).

---

## Time and Dates

**#75. Wie spät ist es?** — What time is it?
*Example sentence:* Entschuldigung, wie spät ist es? — Es ist halb drei. (Excuse me, what time is it? — It is half past two.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Entschuldigung* (Excuse me) + *wie spät ist es?* (how late is it? -> what time is it?) + *Es ist* (It is) + *halb drei* (half [an hour before] three -> 2:30).
  - *Literal translation:* "Excuse me, how late is it? — It is half three."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Excuse me, what time is it? — It is half past two (2:30)."
  - *Context:* Inquiring and telling time; vital distinction that *halb drei* means 2:30, not 3:30.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative adverbial phrase *Wie spät* + copula *ist* in V2 + dummy subject *es*. The answer *Es ist halb drei* illustrates German time reckoning: *halb drei* means half an hour before three (2:30), not 3:30.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 & Hören. Major A1 examination trap: in German, "halb [X]" always indicates thirty minutes before hour [X] (e.g., *halb vier* = 03:30 / 15:30).

**#76. Um wie viel Uhr?** — At what time?
*Example sentence:* Um wie viel Uhr beginnt der Film? (At what time does the movie start?)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Um wie viel Uhr* (At how much hour -> At what time) + *beginnt* (begins/starts) + *der Film?* (the film/movie? [masculine]).
  - *Literal translation:* "At how much clock begins the film?"
  - *Natural meaning:* "At what time does the movie start?"
  - *Context:* Asking for the exact starting time of an event or cinema screening.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Prepositional question phrase *Um wie viel Uhr* (*um* is the mandatory temporal preposition for precise clock times) + finite verb *beginnt* in V2 + subject *der Film* (Nominative masculine).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (prompt cards with clocks or time icons) and Hören (catching event starting times).

**#77. Welcher Tag ist heute?** — What day is today?
*Example sentence:* Welcher Tag ist heute? — Heute ist Montag. (What day is today? — Today is Monday.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Welcher Tag* (Which day [masculine]) + *ist heute?* (is today?) + *Heute ist Montag* (Today is Monday).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "What day is today? — Today is Monday."
  - *Context:* Asking and naming the day of the week.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Interrogative determiner *Welcher* (Nominative masculine modifying *Tag*) + copula *ist* in V2 + temporal adverb *heute*. The response places *Heute* in position 1, causing verb-subject inversion (*ist Montag*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 and Hören. Complete mastery of the 7 days of the week (Montag, Dienstag, Mittwoch, Donnerstag, Freitag, Samstag/Sonnabend, Sonntag) is required.

**#78. Heute ist der…** — Today is the…
*Example sentence:* Heute ist der dritte Mai. (Today is the third of May.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Heute ist* (Today is) + *der dritte Mai* (the third [of] May [masculine nominative ordinal]).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "Today is the third of May."
  - *Context:* Stating the calendar date in full sentences.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Temporal adverb *Heute* (position 1) + copula *ist* in V2 + ordinal number preceded by masculine definite article in the Nominative case (*der dritte Mai*, ordinal suffix *-te* for numbers 1–19).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 (when asked for the exam date) and Schreiben (dating letters and forms: e.g., *Berlin, den 3. Mai*).

**#79. Wann hast du Geburtstag?** — When is your birthday?
*Example sentence:* Wann hast du Geburtstag? — Am 15. Juni. (When is your birthday? — On June 15th.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wann hast du* (When have you [informal]) + *Geburtstag?* (birthday?) + *Am 15. Juni* (On-the [an + dem] 15th of June [dative ordinal *fünfzehnten*]).
  - *Literal translation:* "When hast thou birthday? — At-the 15th June."
  - *Natural meaning:* "When is your birthday? — On June 15th."
  - *Context:* Exchanging personal biographical information with a conversation partner.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Temporal question word *Wann* + finite verb *hast* in V2 + subject *du* + direct object *Geburtstag*. Response features temporal contraction *Am* (*an dem*, Dative) + ordinal number with dative suffix (*15.* -> *fünfzehnten*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 1 & 2. Dates with preposition *am* must take the ordinal suffix *-ten* (e.g., *am ersten*, *am fünfzehnten*).

**#80. Ich habe einen Termin um… Uhr.** — I have an appointment at… o'clock.
*Example sentence:* Ich habe einen Termin um 14 Uhr beim Arzt. (I have an appointment at 2 pm at the doctor's.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Ich habe* (I have) + *einen Termin* (an appointment [masculine accusative]) + *um 14 Uhr* (at 14:00 / 2:00 PM) + *beim Arzt* (at-the [bei + dem] doctor [dative masculine]).
  - *Literal translation:* "I have an appointment at 14 clock by-the doctor."
  - *Natural meaning:* "I have an appointment at 2:00 PM at the doctor's office."
  - *Context:* Discussing scheduled appointments for writing tasks or oral exam planning.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Ich* + transitive verb *habe* in V2 + direct object in Accusative masculine (*einen Termin*) + preposition *um* (exact time) + contraction *beim* (*bei dem*, Dative masculine: *der Arzt* -> *dem Arzt*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2/3 and Schreiben (scheduling, rescheduling, or cancelling medical and administrative appointments).

---

## Weather

**#81. Wie ist das Wetter heute?** — What is the weather like today?
*Example sentence:* Wie ist das Wetter heute? — Es ist sonnig. (What is the weather like today? — It is sunny.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Wie ist* (How is) + *das Wetter* (the weather [neuter]) + *heute?* (today?) + *Es ist sonnig* (It is sunny).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "What is the weather like today? — It is sunny."
  - *Context:* Opening small talk question about current atmospheric conditions.
- **Grammar & Structure:** W-question: interrogative adverb *Wie* + copula *ist* in V2 + subject *das Wetter* (Nominative neuter) + temporal adverb *heute*. Response uses impersonal dummy subject *Es* + predicate adjective *sonnig*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (Thema: Wetter / Freizeit prompt cards) and Hören (identifying weather conditions in daily forecasts).

**#82. Es regnet.** — It is raining.
*Example sentence:* Nimm einen Regenschirm mit, es regnet. (Take an umbrella, it is raining.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Nimm ... mit* (Take ... along! [informal singular imperative of *mitnehmen*]) + *einen Regenschirm* (an umbrella [masculine accusative]) + *es regnet* (it rains / it is raining).
  - *Literal translation:* "Take an umbrella along, it rains."
  - *Natural meaning:* "Take an umbrella with you, it's raining."
  - *Context:* Warning a friend or family member about rainy conditions before they step outside.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Weather verb *regnen* with obligatory impersonal dummy subject *Es* in 3rd person singular (*regnet*). First clause is an informal imperative of separable verb *mitnehmen* (*Nimm ... mit*) + direct object in Accusative masculine (*einen Regenschirm*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Hören (weather forecasts) and Sprechen Teil 2. Weather verbs (*regnen*, *schneien*, *gewittern*) strictly take dummy subject *es*.

**#83. Es ist kalt / warm / heiß.** — It is cold / warm / hot.
*Example sentence:* Es ist heute sehr kalt, zieh eine Jacke an! (It is very cold today, put on a jacket!)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Es ist heute* (It is today) + *sehr kalt* (very cold) + *zieh ... an!* (put ... on! [informal imperative of *anziehen*]) + *eine Jacke* (a jacket [feminine accusative]).
  - *Literal translation:* "It is today very cold, pull a jacket on!"
  - *Natural meaning:* "It is very cold today, put on a jacket!"
  - *Context:* Giving practical clothing advice based on freezing winter weather.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Impersonal clause: dummy subject *Es* + copula *ist* in V2 + temporal adverb *heute* + degree adverb *sehr* + predicate adjective *kalt*. Followed by informal imperative of separable verb *anziehen* (*Zieh ... an!*) + Accusative feminine object (*eine Jacke*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 and Hören. Note the distinction: *Es ist kalt* refers to weather. To say you personally feel cold, use Dative: *Mir ist kalt* (never *Ich bin kalt*, which means emotionally cold).

**#84. Es schneit.** — It is snowing.
*Example sentence:* Es schneit draußen! Die Kinder sind glücklich. (It is snowing outside! The children are happy.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Es schneit* (It is snowing) + *draußen!* (outside!) + *Die Kinder* (The children [plural]) + *sind* (are) + *glücklich* (happy).
  - *Literal & natural meaning:* "It is snowing outside! The children are happy."
  - *Context:* Describing snowy weather and seasonal moods in reading passages and dialogues.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Impersonal verb *schneien* with dummy subject *Es* in 3rd person singular + spatial adverb *draußen*. Second clause has plural subject *Die Kinder* + copula *sind* in V2 + predicate adjective *glücklich*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Hören and Leseverstehen (short seasonal cards, postcards, holiday trip descriptions).

**#85. Die Sonne scheint.** — The sun is shining.
*Example sentence:* Die Sonne scheint, lass uns in den Park gehen. (The sun is shining, let's go to the park.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  *Die Sonne* (The sun [feminine]) + *scheint* (shines / is shining) + *lass uns* (let us [hortative imperative]) + *in den Park* (into the park [accusative masculine movement]) + *gehen* (go).
  - *Literal translation:* "The sun shines, let us into the park go."
  - *Natural meaning:* "The sun is shining, let's go to the park."
  - *Context:* Proposing an outdoor leisure activity triggered by good weather.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject *Die Sonne* (Nominative feminine) + intransitive verb *scheint* in V2 (3rd person singular of *scheinen*). Second clause uses informal hortative imperative *lass uns* + two-way preposition *in* governing directional Accusative masculine (*den Park*) + infinitive *gehen* at clause end.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Goethe A1 Sprechen Teil 2 (suggesting outdoor leisure activities based on favorable weather cards) and Hören.

---

## Health and Body

**#86. Ich bin krank.** — I am sick.
*Example sentence:* Ich bin krank und bleibe heute zu Hause. (I am sick and am staying home today.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  - *Ich bin krank* = I am sick (*ich* = I, *bin* = am [from *sein*], *krank* = sick/ill).
  - *und* = and [coordinating conjunction].
  - *bleibe heute zu Hause* = [I] am staying home today (*bleibe* = stay/remain [from *bleiben*], *heute* = today, *zu Hause* = at home [fixed German idiom for being stationary at home; contrast with *nach Hause* = going homeward]).
  - *Full meaning:* "I am sick and am staying home today." A standard phrase when calling in sick to school or work.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject (*ich*) + copula verb *sein* in position 2 (*bin*) + predicate adjective (*krank*). Regular SVO order.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Essential for Goethe A1 *Schreiben Teil 2* (writing an apology note or cancellation letter to a teacher/school/employer: *"Ich kann morgen nicht kommen, weil ich krank bin."* or *"Ich bin krank und kann nicht zum Deutschkurs kommen."*).

**#87. Mir tut der Kopf weh.** — My head hurts. / I have a headache.
*Example sentence:* Mir tut der Kopf weh, ich brauche eine Tablette. (My head hurts, I need a pill.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  - *Mir tut der Kopf weh* = My head hurts / I have a headache (Lit. "To-me does the head hurt": *mir* = to me [dative personal pronoun], *der Kopf* = the head [grammatical subject in nominative], *tut ... weh* = hurts/aches [separable verb *wehtun*]). German uses dative experiencer + body part as subject to describe bodily aches.
  - *ich brauche eine Tablette* = I need a pill/tablet (*ich* = I, *brauche* = need, *eine Tablette* = a pill [feminine accusative object]).
  - *Full meaning:* "My head hurts, I need a pill." Everyday health complaint and pharmacy request.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Dative experiencer pronoun in position 1 (*mir*) + conjugated separable verb *wehtun* (stem *tut* in position 2, prefix *weh* at sentence end) + subject in nominative (*der Kopf*). Notice singular agreement with *der Kopf*.
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** High-frequency in *Sprechen Teil 2* (flashcards with topic "Gesundheit") and *Hören Teil 1* (identifying health complaints at the doctor's office).

**#88. Ich brauche einen Arzt.** — I need a doctor.
*Example sentence:* Ich fühle mich schlecht, ich brauche einen Arzt. (I feel bad, I need a doctor.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  - *Ich fühle mich schlecht* = I feel bad / unwell (*ich* = I, *fühle mich* = feel myself [reflexive verb *sich fühlen* + accusative reflexive pronoun *mich*], *schlecht* = bad/unwell).
  - *ich brauche einen Arzt* = I need a doctor (*ich* = I, *brauche* = need, *einen Arzt* = a doctor [masculine accusative with *-en*]).
  - *Full meaning:* "I feel bad, I need a doctor." Essential emergency and medical request.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Subject (*ich*) + transitive verb *brauchen* (position 2, regular 1st person *-e*) + direct object in masculine accusative (*einen Arzt*, masculine accusative indefinite article *-en*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Critical emergency phrase for *Sprechen Teil 3* (asking for help/making requests with flashcards) and emergency situations in Germany.

**#89. Mir ist schlecht.** — I feel nauseous / sick.
*Example sentence:* Mir ist schlecht, ich kann nichts essen. (I feel nauseous, I can't eat anything.)
- **Sentence Breakdown & Meaning:**
  - *Mir ist schlecht* = I feel nauseous / sick (Lit. "To-me is bad": *mir* = to me [dative pronoun], *ist* = is, *schlecht* = bad/nauseous). Note the cultural distinction: *Mir ist schlecht* expresses nausea/illness, whereas *Ich bin schlecht* would mean "I am an evil/unskilled person".
  - *ich kann nichts essen* = I cannot eat anything (*ich* = I, *kann* = can [modal *können*], *nichts* = nothing / not anything, *essen* = to eat [infinitive at clause end]).
  - *Full meaning:* "I feel sick/nauseous, I can't eat anything." Describing stomach issues or food-related sickness.
- **Grammar & Structure:** Impersonal construction with dative pronoun (*mir*) + copula *ist* (position 2) + adjective (*schlecht*). No formal grammatical subject is needed, or dummy *es* is dropped when *mir* comes first (*Es ist mir schlecht* -> *Mir ist schlecht*).
- **Exam & Usage Tip:** Tested in *Hören Teil 1/2* dialogues when patients describe symptoms to doctors or pharmacists.

**#90. Ich habe Fieber.** — I have a fever.
