## Comprehensive Answer Key with Detailed Explanations

---

### Section 1: Verb Conjugation (Items 1–20)

**1. lerne**
- *Full sentence:* Ich **lerne** jeden Tag Deutsch.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **ich** (1st person singular). Regular verb **lernen** drops `-en` leaving the stem `lern-`. Adding the standard 1st person singular ending `-e` produces **lerne**.

**2. spielst**
- *Full sentence:* Du **spielst** gern Fußball.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **du** (2nd person singular informal). Regular verb **spielen** has the stem `spiel-`. The 2nd person singular ending is `-st`, giving **spielst**.

**3. läuft**
- *Full sentence:* Er **läuft** sehr schnell.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **er** (3rd person singular). **Laufen** is a strong stem-changing verb: in 2nd and 3rd person singular, stem vowel `au` shifts to `äu` (`lauf-` → `läuf-`). Adding the 3rd person singular ending `-t` results in **läuft**.

**4. kochen**
- *Full sentence:* Wir **kochen** zusammen Abendessen.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **wir** (1st person plural). The regular verb **kochen** takes the plural ending `-en`, which is identical to the infinitive: **kochen**.

**5. lest**
- *Full sentence:* Ihr **lest** viele Bücher.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **ihr** (2nd person plural informal). Notice that stem changes in strong verbs (like `e` → `ie` in *lesen*) occur **only** in the *du* and *er/sie/es* forms (*du liest*, *er liest*). The *ihr* form retains the original base stem `les-` plus `-t` → **lest**.

**6. trinkt**
- *Full sentence:* Sie **trinkt** ein Glas Wasser.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **sie** (3rd person singular, feminine "she"). Regular verb pattern in the present tense: stem `trink-` + 3rd person singular ending `-t` → **trinkt**.

**7. komme**
- *Full sentence:* Ich **komme** aus der Türkei.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **ich** (1st person singular). Base verb **kommen** loses `-en` leaving `komm-` + ending `-e` → **komme**. Essential Goethe A1 introductory sentence pattern.

**8. sprichst**
- *Full sentence:* Du **sprichst** sehr gut Deutsch.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **du** (2nd person singular informal). **Sprechen** is an irregular stem-changing verb with an `e` → `i` shift in the singular 2nd and 3rd persons (`sprech-` → `sprich-`). Adding `-st` produces **sprichst**.

**9. isst**
- *Full sentence:* Er **isst** den Apfel.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **er** (3rd person singular). **Essen** is an irregular stem-changing verb: `e` shifts to `i` (`ess-` → `iss-`). Because the stem ends in double `-s`, the regular 3rd person ending `-t` is appended directly to make **isst** (not three s's). Note: *er isst* (eats) vs *er ist* (is).

**10. fährt**
- *Full sentence:* Mein Vater **fährt** jeden Tag mit dem Auto zur Arbeit.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **mein Vater** (equivalent to 3rd person singular *er*). **Fahren** is a strong verb with an `a` → `ä` vowel mutation in 2nd and 3rd person singular (`fahr-` → `fähr-`). Adding `-t` yields **fährt**.

**11. sehen**
- *Full sentence:* Wir **sehen** heute Abend einen Film.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **wir** (1st person plural). Stem changes only apply to *du* and *er/sie/es*. Plural pronouns (*wir*, *sie*, *Sie*) take the base infinitive form with ending `-en` → **sehen**.

**12. Bist**
- *Full sentence:* **Bist** du müde?
- *Explanation:* A yes/no question starting with the verb (Position 1). The verb is **sein** (to be), which is fully irregular. For the subject **du**, the correct present form is **bist**. Capitalized at the start of a sentence: **Bist**.

**13. habe**
- *Full sentence:* Ich **habe** zwei Kinder.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **ich**. The auxiliary/possession verb **haben** takes the 1st person singular regularized form **habe** (stem `hab-` + `-e`).

**14. schläft**
- *Full sentence:* Das Kind **schläft** schon um 8 Uhr.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **das Kind** (neuter noun, 3rd person singular *es*). **Schlafen** undergoes an `a` → `ä` umlaut shift in the singular stem (`schlaf-` → `schläf-`) + ending `-t` → **schläft**.

**15. Habt**
- *Full sentence:* **Habt** ihr morgen Zeit?
- *Explanation:* Yes/no question with subject **ihr** (2nd person plural). The verb **haben** conjugated for *ihr* takes stem `hab-` + `-t` → **habt**. Capitalized at sentence onset: **Habt**.

**16. geben**
- *Full sentence:* Sie **geben** den Kindern Geschenke.
- *Explanation:* The subject is **Sie** / **sie** (3rd person plural "they"). Plural forms do not undergo stem changes; they take the full infinitive ending `-en` → **geben**.

**17. stehe … auf**
- *Full sentence:* Ich **stehe** um 6 Uhr **auf**.
- *Explanation:* The verb is the separable verb **aufstehen** (prefix `auf-` + base verb `stehen`). In a main clause, the prefix detaches and moves to the very end of the sentence (*Satzklammer* / sentence bracket). The base verb `stehen` conjugates for **ich** in Position 2 → **stehe**.

**18. ruft … an**
- *Full sentence:* Er **ruft** seine Freundin **an**.
- *Explanation:* Separable verb **anrufen** (`an-` + `rufen`). The prefix `an` is placed at the end of the clause. The base stem `ruf-` conjugates for 3rd person singular **er** by adding `-t` in Position 2 → **ruft**.

**19. kaufen … ein**
- *Full sentence:* Wir **kaufen** im Supermarkt **ein**.
- *Explanation:* Separable verb **einkaufen** (`ein-` + `kaufen`). The prefix `ein` detaches to the sentence terminal position. Base verb `kaufen` conjugates for **wir** in Position 2 → **kaufen**.

**20. Siehst … fern**
- *Full sentence:* **Siehst** du abends **fern**?
- *Explanation:* Separable verb **fernsehen** (`fern-` + `sehen`) in a yes/no question. In questions without question words, the conjugated base verb takes Position 1: **sehen** has an `e` → `ie` stem change for **du** → **siehst**. The prefix `fern` goes to the very end of the question.

---

### Section 2: Sentence Reordering (Items 21–35)

**21. Ich höre gern Musik.**
- *Explanation:* In standard declarative main clauses, the conjugated verb must occupy **Position 2** (V2 rule). Here, Subject **Ich** is in Position 1, verb **höre** in Position 2, adverb **gern** (preference) in Position 3, and the direct object **Musik** follows. *(Alternative acceptable inversion: Musik höre ich gern).*

**22. Woher kommst du?**
- *Explanation:* A W-question (open question). Position 1 is reserved for the interrogative pronoun/adverb (**Woher** = from where). Position 2 must strictly be the conjugated finite verb (**kommst**). The subject pronoun follows immediately in Position 3 (**du**).

**23. Die Kinder spielen im Garten.**
- *Explanation:* Standard SVO declaration. Position 1: Subject noun phrase **Die Kinder** (plural). Position 2: conjugated verb **spielen**. Position 3: prepositional location phrase **im Garten** (Dative). *(Alternative: Im Garten spielen die Kinder).*

**24. Morgen fahren wir nach Hamburg.**
- *Explanation:* An inverted main clause emphasizing time. When an adverbial phrase of time (**Morgen**) occupies Position 1, German word order requires subject-verb inversion because the finite verb **fahren** must remain in **Position 2**. The subject **wir** moves directly to Position 3, followed by destination **nach Hamburg**. *(Standard order "Wir fahren morgen nach Hamburg" is also grammatically correct).*

**25. Er trinkt jeden Morgen Kaffee.**
- *Explanation:* Subject **Er** (Pos. 1) + conjugated verb **trinkt** (Pos. 2) + temporal adverbial accusative phrase **jeden Morgen** (Time) + direct object **Kaffee**. Adverbials of time typically precede unspecific noun objects.

**26. Kannst du Deutsch sprechen?**
- *Explanation:* A modal yes/no question. The conjugated modal verb **Kannst** takes Position 1. The subject **du** takes Position 2. Any objects or modifiers (**Deutsch**) follow, while the dependent main verb in infinitive form (**sprechen**) is sent to the very end of the clause, forming the German modal sentence bracket (*Modalverbklammer*).

**27. Ich stehe um 7 Uhr auf.**
- *Explanation:* Separable verb **aufstehen**. Position 1: Subject **Ich**. Position 2: conjugated base verb **stehe**. Middle field: time expression **um 7 Uhr**. Clause-final position: detached prefix **auf**.

**28. Das Buch ist sehr interessant.**
- *Explanation:* Subject **Das Buch** (neuter noun, Pos. 1) + copular verb **ist** (Pos. 2) + predicate adjective phrase with intensifier **sehr interessant** (Pos. 3). Predicate adjectives following *sein* do not take adjective endings.

**29. Meine Schwester wohnt in Berlin.**
- *Explanation:* Subject phrase **Meine Schwester** (Pos. 1) + conjugated verb **wohnt** (Pos. 2) + prepositional phrase of location **in Berlin** (Pos. 3).

**30. Am Samstag gehen wir ins Kino.**
- *Explanation:* Inverted declaration beginning with a temporal prepositional phrase **Am Samstag** in Position 1. The verb **gehen** remains strictly in Position 2. The subject **wir** inverts to Position 3, followed by directional goal phrase **ins Kino** (*in das Kino*, Accusative). *(Or: Wir gehen am Samstag ins Kino).*

**31. Die Lehrerin spricht sehr laut.**
- *Explanation:* Subject **Die Lehrerin** (Pos. 1) + verb **spricht** (Pos. 2, `e` → `i` stem change) + adverbial phrase of manner **sehr laut** (Pos. 3).

**32. Was machst du am Wochenende?**
- *Explanation:* W-question structure: Question word **Was** (Pos. 1) + conjugated finite verb **machst** (Pos. 2) + subject pronoun **du** (Pos. 3) + temporal prepositional phrase **am Wochenende** (Pos. 4) + question mark.

**33. Mein Freund hat einen Hund.**
- *Explanation:* Subject phrase **Mein Freund** (Nominative masculine, Pos. 1) + verb **hat** (Pos. 2) + direct object **einen Hund** (masculine singular in Accusative case: *ein* → *einen*).

**34. Heute muss ich arbeiten.**
- *Explanation:* Inverted modal clause. Temporal adverb **Heute** is in Position 1. Finite modal verb **muss** is in Position 2. Subject **ich** moves to Position 3. The main infinitive verb **arbeiten** is placed at the very end of the sentence. *(Or: Ich muss heute arbeiten).*

**35. Die Kinder machen nach der Schule ihre Hausaufgaben.**
- *Explanation:* Subject **Die Kinder** (Pos. 1) + verb **machen** (Pos. 2) + temporal prepositional phrase **nach der Schule** (Dative feminine) + Accusative direct object **ihre Hausaufgaben** (plural). Temporal expressions typically precede specific direct objects.

---

### Section 3: Negation Exercises (Items 36–50)

**36. Ich habe keinen Bruder.**
- *Explanation:* The positive sentence has an indefinite noun object with article (*einen Bruder*). In German, nouns preceded by *ein* are negated using forms of **kein**. Because *Bruder* is masculine singular and serves as the direct object (Accusative), *kein* takes the accusative ending `-en` → **keinen**.

**37. Sie kommt morgen nicht.**
- *Explanation:* The negation modifies the verb action / entire clause (*she is not coming*). Because there is no indefinite noun or zero-article noun, **nicht** must be used. When negating a specific time or the whole clause, *nicht* is placed at the end of the sentence or after the time adverbial: **morgen nicht**.

**38. Er trinkt keinen Kaffee.**
- *Explanation:* In the positive sentence, *Kaffee* appears with a "zero article" (uncounted mass noun). Nouns with zero articles are negated with **kein-**. Since *Kaffee* is masculine singular (*der Kaffee*) and is the direct object (Accusative), the article is **keinen**.

**39. Das Wetter ist nicht gut.**
- *Explanation:* The element being negated is the predicate adjective **gut**. Adjectives and adverbs are negated with **nicht**, which sits directly before the adjective it negates: **nicht gut**.

**40. Wir haben kein Auto.**
- *Explanation:* The positive sentence contains an indefinite article neuter noun (*ein Auto*). To negate it, substitute *ein* with the corresponding neuter form of **kein**: Nominative/Accusative neuter ending is uninflected → **kein Auto**.

**41. Ich verstehe die Frage nicht.**
- *Explanation:* The object is preceded by a **definite article** (*die Frage*). Specific nouns with definite articles or possessives are negated with **nicht** (not *kein*). To negate the whole verb phrase/clause, **nicht** is placed at the end of the sentence, after the definite direct object.

**42. Er hat keine Kinder.**
- *Explanation:* *Kinder* is a plural noun with no article in the positive statement. Plural zero-article nouns are negated using **keine** (plural ending `-e`).

**43. Sie spricht nicht Deutsch. / Sie spricht kein Deutsch.**
- *Explanation:* Both forms are accepted at A1. When treating *Deutsch* as an adverbial language skill (speaking German), **nicht** is used (*Sie spricht nicht Deutsch*). When treating *Deutsch* as an uncounted noun (German language), **kein** is used (*Sie spricht kein Deutsch*).

**44. Ich kaufe keine Zeitung.**
- *Explanation:* The positive sentence contains an indefinite feminine noun (*eine Zeitung*). Negating an indefinite feminine noun in the Accusative requires the feminine ending `-e`: **keine Zeitung**.

**45. Das ist nicht mein Buch.**
- *Explanation:* The noun is preceded by a **possessive article** (*mein Buch*). Nouns with possessives cannot take *kein*; they must be negated with **nicht**, positioned directly before the possessive phrase: **nicht mein Buch**.

**46. Wir gehen heute nicht ins Kino.**
- *Explanation:* Negates a prepositional directional destination (*ins Kino*). Prepositional phrases are negated by placing **nicht** directly in front of the prepositional phrase (*nicht ins Kino*), following any time adverbials (*heute*).

**47. Er hat kein Geld.**
- *Explanation:* *Geld* is an uncountable neuter noun (*das Geld*) with zero article. It is negated with **kein** (neuter accusative has no added ending) → **kein Geld**.

**48. Sie isst kein Fleisch.**
- *Explanation:* *Fleisch* is a neuter mass noun (*das Fleisch*) appearing without an article. Uncounted food items with zero articles are negated with **kein** → **kein Fleisch**.

**49. Ich kenne den Mann nicht.**
- *Explanation:* The direct object has a definite article (*den Mann*, Accusative masculine). Therefore **kein** cannot be used. Negation of a specific definite object requires **nicht** placed at the end of the clause.

**50. Der Zug kommt nicht pünktlich.**
- *Explanation:* The word being negated is the adverb/adjective **pünktlich** (punctual/on time). To negate an adjective or adverb, place **nicht** directly before it: **nicht pünktlich**.

---

### Section 4: Modal Verb Exercises (Items 51–60)

**51. kann**
- *Full sentence:* Ich **kann** heute nicht kommen.
- *Explanation:* Modal verb **können** (to be able to). For 1st person singular **ich**, the modal undergoes a stem-vowel change (`ö` → `a`) and drops personal endings: *ich kann* (identical to *er/sie/es kann*). The main verb **kommen** stands in the infinitive at the end.

**52. musst**
- *Full sentence:* Du **musst** deine Hausaufgaben machen.
- *Explanation:* Modal verb **müssen** (must/have to). For 2nd person singular **du**, the umlaut is dropped (`ü` → `u`), and personal ending `-st` is added: **musst**. Dependent infinitive **machen** brackets at the sentence end.

**53. möchte**
- *Full sentence:* Er **möchte** ein Eis essen.
- *Explanation:* Subjunctive/polite modal form **möchten** (would like to). For 3rd person singular **er/sie/es**, the form is **möchte** (note: 1st and 3rd person singular forms are identical for all modal verbs). Infinitive **essen** is placed clause-finally.

**54. Dürfen**
- *Full sentence:* **Dürfen** wir hier parken?
- *Explanation:* Modal verb **dürfen** (to be allowed to / may). For 1st person plural **wir**, modal verbs keep their plural stem (with umlaut) and end in `-en`: **dürfen**. Occupies Position 1 in a yes/no question. Capitalized: **Dürfen**.

**55. sollt**
- *Full sentence:* Ihr **sollt** leise sein.
- *Explanation:* Modal verb **sollen** (should / to be supposed to). Conjugated for 2nd person plural **ihr**: stem `soll-` + regular plural ending `-t` → **sollt**. Copula infinitive **sein** stands at the end.

**56. will**
- *Full sentence:* Sie **will** Ärztin werden.
- *Explanation:* Modal verb **wollen** (to want to). For 3rd person singular feminine (*sie* = she), the vowel shifts from `o` to `i`, taking no personal suffix: **will** (*ich will*, *er/sie/es will*). Dependent infinitive **werden** is at the end.

**57. Kannst**
- *Full sentence:* **Kannst** du mir helfen?
- *Explanation:* Modal verb **können** for 2nd person singular **du**: vowel shift `ö` → `a` + ending `-st` → **kannst**. Standard Goethe A1 polite request in oral examination (Sprechen Teil 3). Capitalized: **Kannst**.

**58. dürfen**
- *Full sentence:* Die Kinder **dürfen** nicht so viel Schokolade essen.
- *Explanation:* Subject **Die Kinder** is 3rd person plural (they). Plural modals retain their infinitive form: **dürfen**. Infinitive **essen** is at the end.

**59. möchte**
- *Full sentence:* Ich **möchte** ein Glas Wasser, bitte.
- *Explanation:* **Möchten** for 1st person singular **ich** is **möchte**. In everyday ordering contexts (restaurant/café), the dependent verb *haben* or *trinken* is frequently omitted when the accusative object is explicit.

**60. darf**
- *Full sentence:* Man **darf** hier nicht rauchen.
- *Explanation:* The impersonal pronoun **man** (one / people in general) always takes **3rd person singular** agreement. For **dürfen**, the 3rd person singular undergoes stem change `ü` → `a` with zero ending: **darf**. Crucial for signs and prohibitions in Goethe A1 Lesen.

---

### Section 5: Article / Gender Exercises (Items 61–70)

**61. Die**
- *Full sentence:* **Die** Frau liest ein Buch.
- *Explanation:* *Frau* is feminine (*die Frau*). It functions here as the grammatical subject of the sentence, which requires the **Nominative** case. Definite article for feminine Nominative is **Die**.

**62. den**
- *Full sentence:* Ich sehe **den** Mann.
- *Explanation:* *Mann* is masculine (*der Mann*). The verb *sehen* governs a direct object in the **Accusative** case. In German, only masculine articles change form in the Accusative: *der* becomes **den**.

**63. Das**
- *Full sentence:* **Das** Kind spielt im Garten.
- *Explanation:* *Kind* is neuter (*das Kind*). Functioning as the subject in the **Nominative** case, the definite neuter article is **Das**.

**64. eine**
- *Full sentence:* Er kauft **eine** Zeitung.
- *Explanation:* *Zeitung* is feminine (*die Zeitung*). Functioning as the direct object (Accusative) of the transitive verb *kaufen*. Feminine indefinite article remains **eine** in both Nominative and Accusative cases.

**65. einen**
- *Full sentence:* Ich habe **einen** Hund.
- *Explanation:* *Hund* is masculine (*der Hund*). The verb *haben* always triggers the **Accusative** case for its direct object. The masculine indefinite article *ein* inflects with the accusative suffix `-en` → **einen**.

**66. Die**
- *Full sentence:* **Die** Schule ist groß.
- *Explanation:* *Schule* is feminine (*die Schule*). As the subject of the copular verb *ist*, it takes the **Nominative** case. The definite article is **Die**.

**67. einen**
- *Full sentence:* Wir brauchen **einen** Tisch.
- *Explanation:* *Tisch* is masculine (*der Tisch*). The transitive verb *brauchen* requires an **Accusative** direct object. The indefinite article changes from *ein* to **einen**.

**68. den**
- *Full sentence:* Sie trinkt **den** Kaffee.
- *Explanation:* *Kaffee* is masculine (*der Kaffee*). When referring to a specific coffee with a definite article in the **Accusative** (direct object of *trinken*), masculine *der* becomes **den**.

**69. eine**
- *Full sentence:* Ich suche **eine** Wohnung.
- *Explanation:* *Wohnung* is feminine (*die Wohnung*). As the direct object of *suchen* (Accusative), the indefinite article takes the feminine form **eine**.

**70. Das**
- *Full sentence:* **Das** Buch ist sehr interessant.
- *Explanation:* *Buch* is neuter (*das Buch*). Functioning as the subject in the **Nominative** case, the definite article is **Das**.

---

### Section 6: Perfect Tense Exercises (Items 71–80)

**71. Ich habe Deutsch gelernt.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **haben** is used because *lernen* is a regular transitive verb without movement or change of condition. Conjugated for *ich*: **habe** (Position 2).
  - *Partizip II formation:* *lernen* is a regular (weak) verb. Prefix `ge-` + stem `lern-` + suffix `-t` = **gelernt**.
  - *Word order:* The Partizip II is placed at the absolute end of the sentence (*Satzklammer*).

**72. Er hat Fußball gespielt.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **haben** is used for games/sports activities. Conjugated for *er*: **hat** (Position 2).
  - *Partizip II formation:* *spielen* is a regular weak verb: `ge-` + stem `spiel-` + suffix `-t` = **gespielt**.
  - *Word order:* Object *Fußball* sits in the middle field; *gespielt* closes the bracket at the end.

**73. Wir sind ins Kino gegangen.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **sein** is strictly required because *gehen* denotes a change of location / physical movement from one place to another. Conjugated for *wir*: **sind** (Position 2).
  - *Partizip II formation:* *gehen* is an irregular (strong) verb with vowel change: `ge-` + altered stem `gang-` + `-en` = **gegangen**.

**74. Sie ist nach München gefahren.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **sein** is required because *fahren* involves directional vehicular motion. Conjugated for 3rd person singular *sie* (she): **ist** (Position 2).
  - *Partizip II formation:* *fahren* is a strong verb: `ge-` + stem `fahr-` + `-en` = **gefahren** (retains vowel *a* in Partizip II).

**75. Ich habe ein Buch gekauft.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **haben** is used for commercial transactions / transitive verbs taking direct accusative objects. Conjugated for *ich*: **habe**.
  - *Partizip II formation:* *kaufen* is a regular weak verb: `ge-` + stem `kauf-` + suffix `-t` = **gekauft**.

**76. Du hast einen Brief geschrieben.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **haben** is used for transitive actions with direct objects. Conjugated for *du*: **hast**.
  - *Partizip II formation:* *schreiben* is a strong verb with vowel ablaut (`ei` → `ie`): `ge-` + stem `schrieb-` + `-en` = **geschrieben**.

**77. Er hat einen Apfel gegessen.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **haben** is used for consumption verbs (eating/drinking). Conjugated for *er*: **hat**.
  - *Partizip II formation:* *essen* is an irregular strong verb: prefix `ge-` + `g-` insertion + stem `ess-` + `-en` = **gegessen**.

**78. Wir haben Kaffee getrunken.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **haben** for beverage consumption. Conjugated for *wir*: **haben**.
  - *Partizip II formation:* *trinken* is a strong verb with vowel ablaut (`i` → `u`): `ge-` + stem `trunk-` + `-en` = **getrunken**.

**79. Sie sind um 8 Uhr gekommen.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **sein** is required because *kommen* indicates arrival / movement towards a destination. Conjugated for *sie* (they): **sind**.
  - *Partizip II formation:* *kommen* is a strong verb: `ge-` + stem `komm-` + `-en` = **gekommen** (stem vowel remains *o*).

**80. Ich bin zu Hause geblieben.**
- *Explanation:*
  - *Auxiliary choice:* **sein** is required. Even though *bleiben* means to stay/remain (no physical movement), in German grammar **bleiben** and **sein** are classic exceptions that grammatically mandate the auxiliary **sein**. Conjugated for *ich*: **bin**.
  - *Partizip II formation:* *bleiben* is a strong verb with vowel ablaut (`ei` → `ie`): `ge-` + stem `blieb-` + `-en` = **geblieben**.
